- **2026-11-02** · ♥ 36 · ↻ 3 — **Article: How sUSDai yield works and the CHIP Hurdle Rate** [post](https://x.com/USDai_Official/status/2117090600142901713)
  > Summary
  > The CHIP Foundation donated 127,927.66 USDai into sUSDai under the CHIP Hurdle Rate (CHR). That brought the yield for someone who deposited on Oct 15, when CHR launched, to a 7% APY through Nov 1, measured just before redemptions were processed. Holders who stayed through the redemption earned about 8.04% over the same period.
  > How long you held matters. Redemptions are paid every 30 days, and the redemption price counts loan interest only once borrowers pay it. If you held through a full 30-day period, you captured that period’s payments and likely earned more than 7%. If you redeemed sooner, some of the interest you earned hadn’t been paid yet, such as payments that landed on Nov 6, after this redemption. That interest stayed in sUSDai and lifted the share price for holders who stayed, which is where the extra above 7% comes from.
  > This post explains why sUSDai yield arrives in steps, how its two share prices keep that fair to everyone, how CHR works from here, and what is changing to make the timing easier to follow.
  > How sUSDai yield works: asynchronous deposits via ERC-7540
  > sUSDai is built on the ERC-7540 asynchronous standard, so deposits and redemptions do not settle at the same price or time. It has two share prices, which count interest at different moments.
  > Deposit share price counts loan and escrow interest as it accrues, every second. New deposits mint at this price.
  > Redemption share price counts loan and escrow interest only once it is paid. Redemptions are paid at this price.
  > Both include base yield on the PYUSD backing as it accrues. Both are net of the 10% protocol fee.
  > Why two prices. sUSDai is backed by loans to GPU operators. They pay interest monthly, not every block. With one price, someone could deposit just before a large payment lands, redeem right after, and take a month of interest earned by others. The two prices and the 30-day redemption cycle stop that and protect long-term holders.
  > The trade-off. Between payment dates, the redemption price trails the deposit price by up to a month of interest. Hold through a full cycle and you capture it. Redeem earlier, and interest that built up during your stay but wasn’t yet paid stays in sUSDai. As borrowers pay it, it lifts the share price for holders who stay.
  > The deposit price climbs steadily as interest accrues. The redemption price moves in steps on payment days, then catches up.
  > Example: this period
  > From Oct 15 to Nov 1, the deposit share price rose at a 6.53% annualized rate before any donation. The donation lifted that to 7.00%. The Nov 1 redemption then added about another point for holders who stayed, taking them to about 8.04%.
  > Redeemers were paid at the Nov 1 redemption price (the dashed line). Interest that borrowers paid after that date, including interest earned before Nov 1, lifts the share price only for holders who stayed.
  > The site APY shows what sUSDai earns at any moment, annualized. The period figures show what the share price actually did between Oct 15 and Nov 1. Both sit below 7% partly because base yield fell in mid-October, when large outflows on Oct 15 shrank the PYUSD backing that earns it.
  > How the donation was sized. The donation targeted the deposit share price for someone who deposited on Oct 15, measured at Nov 1, 19:00 UTC, just before the redemption queue was processed.
  > Start price on Oct 15: 1.118800.
  > Target price at a 7% simple annualized rate over 17.79 days: 1.118800 × (1 + 0.07 × 17.79 / 365) = 1.122617.
  > Projected price without a donation: 1.122360, a 6.53% annualized rate.
  > Shortfall per share: 1.122617 − 1.122360 ≈ 0.000257.
  > Donation: shortfall × 497.84M shares = 127,927.66 USDai, sent onchain at 18:54 UTC.
  > Figures are rounded to six decimals. The exact donation was 127,927.658346 USDai.
  > This sizing applied to this month. It was set before counting the interest redeemers left behind, which alone would have taken holders who stayed above 7%. The ongoing CHR mechanism will be finalized in an upcoming governance proposal, open to community input.
  > How CHR works from here
  > CHR is ongoing. Each redemption period, it supports a 7% annualized rate on the deposit share price, subject to available capital. The proposed process, subject to that governance proposal:
  > Measure. Take the deposit share price, which is already net of fees, at the start of the period and again after the redemption queue is processed.
  > Compare and donate. If the annualized change is below 7%, the CHIP Foundation donates the difference into sUSDai from the Yield Reserve, and it’s reflected in the share price.
  > Publish. Each donation is sent onchain as one exact amount.
  > Periods that earn more than 7% get no donation, and holders keep the full yield. The 7% applies to the deposit share price over a whole period, so a holder’s own yield also depends on when they enter and exit.
  > What we are changing
  > Loan payments follow invoices, wires and borrower operations, so they will always arrive in steps. These changes make those steps more predictable.
  > Higher escrow rates. Capital in escrow earns interest while it waits to fund loans, and that interest is part of sUSDai’s yield. Escrow earns 6–8% today, and new escrows are set to earn 10% or more.
  > Borrower payments on the 1st. Borrower payments are moving to the 1st of the month, so they arrive together. This period, some landed as late as Nov 6.
  > Redemptions after payments settle. We’re targeting the 2nd of each month for redemption processing, after the month’s payments land. The contract runs on 30-day periods today, so calendar dates need a contract upgrade first.
  > Smoother redemptions. We’re working with DeFi partners on further improvements to redemptions, planned for the coming months and open to all holders.
  > Closing
  > sUSDai yield comes from GPU loans with monthly payment cycles. The two share prices keep that fair between holders who stay and holders who leave. CHR supports a 7% rate each period, and the changes above make the timing easier to plan around.
  > Disclaimer
  > This post is for information only. It is not investment advice, a recommendation, or an offer or solicitation to buy or sell any digital asset. sUSDai yield is not guaranteed and can be lower, zero or negative. Past yields, including the figures in this post, don’t predict future results. The CHIP Hurdle Rate is not a guaranteed minimum and depends on available capital. Redemptions are processed in 30-day cycles from available cash, and payment can take longer when cash is short. The planned changes described above are targets and may change. sUSDai is not a bank deposit, savings account or bank product. Holding it involves risks including borrower default, collateral losses, enforcement delays, and smart contract and operational failures, and holders can lose principal. This communication is not directed at residents of the United States, the United Kingdom, or other jurisdictions where its distribution may be restricted.

- **2026-10-28** · ♥ 117 · ↻ 14 — **Article: The Compounding Economics of CHIP** [post](https://x.com/USDai_Official/status/2115397454539403512)
  > Velocity builds mass. Mass funds CHIP, and scales the gravity of its USD.AI network.
  > For followers of USD.AI, product-market fit was the 1st hard question, and the origination engine built in Allo Game Season 2 demonstrated that PMF moment. The 2nd hard question, for CHIP holders and sUSDai depositors, is just as simple to state: when the protocol earns, where does the revenue go, and what reaches CHIP?
  > Now that CHIP governance is live, this post answers it line by line. The companion video walks through the same material and the heuristic developed to accommodate the heavy offchain and institutional nature of the business, in collaboration with the onchain network built around the settlement layer.
  > The CHIP protocol (previously USD.AI protocol) is sUSDai, USDai and the contracts that fund and settle every loan, on terms set by CHIP holders. CHIP Foundation (previously USD.AI Foundation) stewards that onchain side. USD.AI Inc. (previously Permian Labs) builds offchain products that make the protocol’s loans cheaper and faster to produce. Each earns a different kind of revenue. That difference decides where the revenue goes, and it is how alignment is demonstrated without sacrificing growth.
  > One rule organizes every decision going forward: Velocity builds mass. Mass funds CHIP. Revenue earned on velocity, each time a loan is made and sold, is reinvested against the friction that slows the loans (sUSDai) & stablecoin supply (USDai) down. Revenue earned on mass, every dollar of USDai and sUSDai outstanding, is routed to CHIP.
  > Proposal summary: The proposal is for all sUSDai net interest margin (NIM), net of CHIP Foundation costs, to go to programmatic CHIP purchases from today. Origination fees and gain on sale (the margin earned when a seasoned loan is sold through GLX) are mostly reinvested in making the next loan cheaper and faster, with some discretionary CHIP purchases. 40% of gain on sale goes to the Yield Reserve, which defends the 7% CHIP Hurdle Rate for sUSDai as loans are sold. USDai NIM is switched off today and is expected to become the largest source of CHIP purchases as credit outstanding grows over time, and more ecosystem products are built around cash settlement of all things compute (platform sales/services). The long-term objective is a net deflationary CHIP: protocol purchases exceeding new issuance.
  > Creating gravity: new loans add mass to the protocol, and a stablecoin for compute settlement is the North Star
  > The initial product of the CHIP protocol is a new GPU financing loan, where a neocloud borrows against its GPUs on standard terms. The loan is funded from sUSDai, and its payments settle in USDai. CHIP holders set the terms every loan must meet: collateral eligibility, rate tiers, fees, and the underwriting parameters being set in a separate proposal. CHIP Foundation stewards the contracts that hold and settle those loans.
  > The loan is the initial product. The stablecoin is the North Star. Every loan originated through sUSDai is denominated in USDai and settles in it. So every loan adds to the mass of USDai and sUSDai outstanding, and the mass is where the durable revenue lives: NIM on USDai & sUSDai. Finance the machines, the machines adopt the money, and that dollar will settle everything in compute & AI. Mass is what pulls & compounds the next loan in.
  > The loan is the issuance mechanism for USDai. Revenue earned on the loan’s motion is reinvested against friction. Revenue earned on the mass is routed to CHIP.
  > Around that initial product, USD.AI Inc. builds the complementary products that raise the velocity of the loan throughput and the quality of new loans. Each one sits on one stage of a loan’s life:
  > Origination (new loan): gpuloans.com brings borrowers to the protocol. This is typically the most expensive part of any debt business: “finance” is consistently one of the most expensive cost-per-click (CPC) categories in paid search.
  > Underwriting: Compute IQ standardizes how each loan is assessed, with a rubric trained on prior deals. The education costs are high due to the nascency of this sector compared to student loans, mortgages, etc.
  > Distribution (loan sale): GLX, the GPU Loans Exchange, sells seasoned loans to institutional buyers so the same capital can be lent again. This is the newest business line of USD.AI Inc., with early adoption by multi-billion-dollar funds, banks and institutions, but many products must still be built to meet their compliance and underwriting rigor.
  > Platform (servicing & value bundling): Loan servicing, the deal manager and the borrower portal today; payments, telemetry, hedging, and other services bundled into settlement over time.
  > None of these products is the protocol, as they almost all exist offchain to make the protocol work. All of them exist to make the protocol’s loans cheaper, faster and better, so that more loans are funded, held and settled onchain.
  > That distinction decides where revenue goes. The fees earned offchain (origination fees, the reinvested share of gain on sale, platform services) are earned by USD.AI Inc. They pay for the products that shrink the cost of the next loan. The revenue earned onchain (NIM on sUSDai, and in time NIM on USDai) is the protocol’s. CHIP holders direct it, and the proposal is to direct it to CHIP purchases.
  > Not every dollar earned in the ecosystem is protocol revenue: there are significant costs in just the origination and structuring of a loan (upward of millions of dollars per loan at times), and the framework should be judged on its defined allocations rather than on the total. But the offchain fees do reach CHIP, one step removed. They are what grow the mass of USDai and sUSDai outstanding, and the mass is the revenue base that CHIP purchases are drawn from. It is the only base that compounds. Sending the fees to CHIP today would buy CHIP once. Spending them on velocity adds mass every year after, and lets the protocol capture the most powerful force in finance: compounding.
  > The trade-off: reinvest for compounding velocity, and purchase CHIP for alignment
  > The first post in this series argued that financing the next 10,000 neoclouds needs a far lower fixed cost per loan. Capital alone does not solve it. Each borrower still has to be found, underwritten, documented, funded and connected to a long-term capital source. The same constraint should shape where revenue goes.
  > Revenue can do two things. It can be reinvested to lower the cost of the next loan and raise future revenue. Or it can purchase CHIP today, so that the protocol relies less on emissions, but revenue stays where it is. Both are good uses. The question is sequencing, and the answer depends on where each dollar is earned.
  > CHIP purchases are a steady-state mechanism, and every dollar used for CHIP cannot be used for new talent, new products and new features. They make sense once a function can sustain itself and earns more than it needs. Applying that policy to every function while the infrastructure is still being built would starve the system that produces and multiplies the revenue. The distinction matters for the scale of the network and for the credibility of its decentralization.
  > A dollar reinvested in a stage that is still expensive does more than a dollar of work. It lowers the cost of every loan that follows. Lower cost raises turnover. Turnover multiplies every fee earned on motion. Amazon’s 1997 shareholder letter committed to investing in customers and infrastructure and judging each dollar by its results; the company then ran close to zero reported profit for most of two decades while it built fulfillment, Prime and AWS. Google’s 2004 founders’ letter explained why it would fund long-term opportunities even when they reduced near-term results. Both reinvested for years before they spent the cash on anything else, and neither would have reached its scale by paying out early. The lesson is about how infrastructure gets built (it does not make CHIP equivalent to shares in those companies, or imply comparable outcomes).
  > …

- **2026-10-20** · ♥ 64 · ↻ 2 — **Article: CHIP Governance Is Live** [post](https://x.com/USDai_Official/status/2112333747445973266)
  > The CHIP Governance Forum is live at governance.usd.ai. It is where the standards for financing compute through sUSDai are proposed, discussed and decided.
  > Every proposal is public, follows the same path, and is put to a CHIP holder vote.
  > What CHIP governance covers
  > Proposals cover how capital moves through sUSDai, from origination to liquidity. The forum is organized into six categories:
  > How proposals work
  > Every proposal follows the same path:
  > CRFC (CHIP Request for Comment). The proposal is posted on the forum for at least 4 days of public discussion, along with any required risk, legal or technical review.
  > Snapshot vote. CHIP holders vote on Snapshot. Rejected proposals stop here.
  > Onchain vote and timelock. Where execution is required, the approved proposal goes to an onchain vote, then a timelock before it takes effect.
  > Implementation. Completed actions and supporting evidence are recorded in the proposal's forum topic.
  > Posts are labeled either Discussion, for early ideas and feedback, or Proposal, for a defined change going to a vote.
  > The first proposal
  > The first proposal sets the origination parameters for new sUSDai financings:
  > Origination criteria: 80% maximum blended portfolio LTV, 1.15x minimum quarterly portfolio DSCR, and a residual value warranty for any non-investment-grade offtaker or on-demand rental
  > Approved collateral: NVIDIA B200, B300, GB200, GB300 and VR200; AMD MI455X and MI430X; non-core inference chips only alongside contracted offtake
  > Maximum utilization: 90% of sUSDai to deploy new financings
  > Underwriting: a minimum Compute IQ score of 60
  > Compute IQ is USD.AI's agentic underwriting system, built on more than $25 billion of GPU financing data. It scores every financing on one 100-point scale across the operator, the customer and the contract, and the score sets facility size, pricing tier and structure.
  > Terms for selling facilities through GLX will be set in a separate governance proposal.
  > How to take part
  > Read the first proposal
  > Read and discuss proposals on the CHIP Governance Forum
  > Vote on Snapshot
  > Start with the Welcome to CHIP Governance post for the full process

- **2026-10-15** · ♥ 53 · ↻ 7 — **Article: 10,000 Neoclouds: Building the Financial Network for Compute** [post](https://x.com/USDai_Official/status/2110536654167453845)
  > USD.AI has reviewed more GPU financing opportunities than almost any other organization in the world.
  > This year to date (October 13th), USD.AI has reviewed nearly 700 deals totalling $25bn worth of loan demand, and is currently scaling its originations volumes with the help of GLX to meet the capital needs of borrowers.
  > Just for reference here’s the same pipeline chart, released only 3 months ago in the Lighthouse YTD Report.
  > Across the board, USD.AI has seen a massive increase in inbound demand.
  > Since USD.AI launched, the borrower mix has shifted twice.  In late 2025, most of the inbound was Bitcoin miners turned AI providers. This year most of the demand has come from general-purpose inference sellers, aka neoclouds, looking to scale or start up.
  > Now a third group is emerging: law firms, biotech labs, research groups, or any companies buying GPUs for their own use to stay strategic with intelligence across data controls to spend management. These companies have hit the scale where owning beats renting. That’s what the USD.AI pipeline shows, and it appears to reflect a wider trend across the industry, as compute becomes a core input to how these companies work, rather than a product they sell.
  > These are likely the borrowers of the future, and the clearest picture of the Compute Middle Market.
  > These end users are quasi-neoclouds. They run chips to serve inference, and they are also the core offtaker.
  > USD.AI’s bet is that there will be 10,000 of these. Not 10,000 copies of today’s neoclouds, but 10,000 companies that own the compute their business runs on. Each is too big to keep renting and too small for anyone on Wall Street to notice.
  > GPU capital markets aren’t built for them. They aren’t hyperscalers; they aren’t building gigawatt-scale deployments with 100,000 GPUs. They need inference, and their biggest problem is the friction around financing a deployment that the market considers small. Small is relative: these deals are $25 million to $250 million, which is a lot of money everywhere except in a GPU capital market sized for hyperscalers.
  > So USD.AI was rebuilt around one mission: making compute loans cheaper and faster to make, move and settle.
  > The rest of this post walks through what that mission changes, from who the borrowers are now and why the bottleneck is credit friction rather than capital, to how the business moves from holding loans to moving and settling them, and finally what it all means for the protocol and sUSDai holders.
  > I. The borrower patterns are changing: 10,000 Neoclouds
  > The Compute Middle Market: from 300 neoclouds to 10,000 borrowers
  > The first wave of neoclouds came out of bitcoin mining, because miners had the one thing AI needed most and couldn’t get quickly: power.
  > The second wave was the general-purpose inference sellers that make up the largest part of the USD.AI pipeline today, companies built from the start to sell GPU capacity, and helped along by modular data centers that make power easier to reach and faster to bring online.
  > Between them, they lowered the bar for running a cluster far enough that a third wave is now showing up in USD.AI’s inbound: end users. So far they are mostly AI-native software and product companies, but the conversations underway reach well beyond that, to law firms, biotech companies, materials science labs and AI safety teams. Companies whose product depends on compute, deciding to own it.
  > For an AI-native company, compute is often the biggest line on the P&L, and when rent is that expensive, owning with a loan starts to look like buying a house instead of renting one. A company running open-weight models on its own hardware also keeps its data in-house, which for a law firm or a lab is not a small consideration.
  > That is the Compute Middle Market: a few hundred neoclouds today, typically borrowing $10-$100 million at a time. Further down the line, there will be 10,000 end-user borrowers at $5-$50 million each. After that come the microclouds, the 100,000 companies for whom a single rack is a cluster.
  > II. Power isn't the bottleneck, it’s the cost-per-loan.
  > The fixed-cost problem in compute credit
  > People assume compute financing is limited by power, or by what a GPU will be worth in three years.
  > Both of those matter, but the actual binding constraint is the cost of making a loan.
  > There is roughly $2 trillion in private credit looking for yield, across banks, credit funds, insurers and asset managers. The problem is getting it to a $20 million borrower at a cost that makes sense.
  > Today a bespoke GPU loan costs $5 million to $7 million to put together, across origination, underwriting, legal, structuring, settlement and distribution. That cost stays about the same whether the loan is $500 million or $20 million, because each deal is diligenced from scratch and negotiated line by line. On a $500 million loan, it's a rounding error. On a $20 million loan, it’s 15 to 25 cents of every dollar, which is not feasible at all for the 10,000 borrowers.
  > GPU ownership is growing faster than credit can be issued against it.
  > SemiAnalysis recently made the same argument from the other end of the market. In their view, the constraint on AI infrastructure has moved from data centers to chips and now to financing.
  > Lenders today will underwrite almost nothing that isn’t investment-grade or backed by an Nvidia or hyperscaler guarantee, and by SemiAnalysis’s estimate, Nvidia’s balance sheet can support around 46 GW of capacity against roughly 240 GW coming online. The top of the market is covered. The compute middle market isn’t, and no amount of backstop capacity will reach a $20 million borrower.
  > Private credit as it exists today is a tailored suit: beautifully made, cut one at a time, and right for the largest borrowers, but impossible to make 10,000 times.
  > Housing solved the same problem with the mortgage backed security. Every house is different, but the mortgage is a standardized debt instrument that can be pooled, and pooling turns a thousand unremarkable borrowers into a security that a bank, an insurer and a pension fund can all buy.
  > And more importantly, pooling loans together is how non-IG credit becomes IG through diversification. A billion dollars lent to one borrower needs Nvidia behind it. A billion dollars lent to a hundred borrowers doesn’t.
  > The end state for what USD.AI is building is the creation of a new ABS market for GPUs, where 50 performing GPU loans can be packaged up and sold to institutional investors through GLX. It’s the natural state of where the market is heading.
  > Power constraints are pushing deployments toward 2 to 5 MW instead of 250 MW, and financing constraints are pushing toward many small loans instead of a few enormous ones. Smaller deployments with lead times of a couple of months can also track actual demand more closely than gigawatt campuses planned years ahead, which makes the whole industry less likely to overbuild.
  > Standardize the loan, and the capital follows. That’s the GPU mortgage.
  > The AI Loan Factory
  > If the binding constraint is the cost of making a loan, then the company has to be built around cutting it.
  > USD.AI broke the life of a loan into five stages and gave each stage one team with one job: make that stage cheaper and faster, and prove it with a number.
  > Origination: gpuloans.com. Inbound demand, qualified before anyone gets on a call, so the team only spends time on borrowers who can close.
  > Underwriting: ComputeIQ. A 100-point rubric calibrated on a pipeline of more than 600 GPU financing deals, which scores operator, offtaker and contract in days rather than weeks.
  > Servicing: the borrower portal on usd.ai. The documents, covenants and payments that run a loan for three years, tracked against live collections and telemetry instead of quarterly spreadsheets.
  > Funding: sUSDai. The capital that funds a loan the day it closes, and the integrations that keep that pool growing.
  > Distribution: GLX. The USD.AI loan marketplace, where seasoned loans are sold on to +8 credit funds with more than $105 billion in combined AUM, so the capital that funded them comes back to fund the next one.

- **2026-10-14** · ♥ 47 · ↻ 9 — **Article: Allo Game: Flatiron Ends, Metropolitan Begins** [post](https://x.com/USDai_Official/status/2110437072448163972)
  > TL;DR
  > Season 3 of the Allo Game, Metropolitan, starts with a 7% minimum yield on sUSDai. If sUSDai yield drops below 7% at any point, the protocol’s Yield Reserve tops it up. This floor is the CHIP Hurdle Rate.
  > Season 2, Flatiron, ends October 14, Metropolitan starts right after, and Season 2 CHIP claims open October 20.
  > Here is how Season 2 went and what changes next.
  > Season 2 ends October 14 at 11:59 p.m. UTC (7:59 p.m. ET). Season 3 starts immediately after.
  > No action needed on positions. Keep qualifying positions where they are and they start earning Season 3 points.
  > 225M CHIP (2.25% of supply) goes to Season 2. Check estimates from October 15, report any issues by October 19, and claim from October 20 to January 31, 2027.
  > Introducing the CHIP Hurdle Rate: starting at a 7% minimum sUSDai yield from Season 3. If yield falls short, the difference is paid from the Yield Reserve. Both the CHIP Hurdle Rate and the Yield Reserve balance will be shown on the app.usd.ai dashboard. This rate may change in the future.
  > Season 3 runs to April 14, 2027, with new multipliers and no referrals.
  > ICO aligned participants in the eight-month Level Up can claim their unlocked CHIP and USDC refund from October 15 on the Vault tab of the CHIP page.
  > Season 2 (Flatiron) rewards and key dates
  > The Season 2 airdrop is 225 million CHIP, or 2.25% of total supply, shared across all Season 2 points. The pool also covers CHIP for integration partners. Unlike Season 1, there is no ICO or airdrop alignment to choose. Every point counts toward the airdrop.
  > Key dates (UTC)
  > Oct 14, 11:59 p.m.: S2 ends. Points stop accruing and a final snapshot is taken. Nothing required. Positions you keep in place start earning Season 3 points right away.
  > Oct 15, 00:00: S3 starts. The app shows each wallet's final S2 points and estimated CHIP airdrop. The review period runs through Oct 19. Check your points and est. CHIP allocation. If something looks off, report issues in the Telegram channel or Support Chat.
  > Oct 15: ICO aligned participants in the eight-month Level Up can claim their unlocked CHIP and USDC refund. Claim both at https://app.usd.ai/chip?tab=claim
  > Oct 19: Review period ends. Final CHIP allocations are published.
  > Oct 20, 15:00 (11 AM ET): S2 airdrop claims open on Arbitrum. No lockup, no vesting. Claim your CHIP in the app.
  > Jan 31, 2027: Claim window closes. Claim before this date. Unclaimed CHIP is forfeited.
  > Apr 14, 2027: Season 3 ends.
  > ICO aligned participants in the eight-month Level Up: from October 15, claim your unlocked CHIP and your USDC refund of the valuation difference on the Vault tab of the CHIP page. This is separate from the Season 2 points airdrop. Four month locks and airdrop participants from Season 1 have nothing left to do. See the Level Up terms for details.
  > Season 2 (Flatiron) by the numbers
  > Season 2 ran from February 19 to October 14, almost eight months. Over that time, GPU loans on the books grew from $6.7 million to $311 million, after peaking at $401 million in late September. That growth was funded by everyone who held, looped and provided liquidity for USDai and sUSDai. Figures below are as of October 14 unless noted.
  > Protocol growth
  > sUSDai TVL grew 64%, from $336 million to $549 million.
  > Total TVL (USDai and sUSDai) rose from $522 million to $660 million. It more than doubled from its $280 million low in early April.
  > sUSDai earns 6.9% net APY today. Season 3 sets a 7% minimum through the CHIP Hurdle Rate (see below).
  > Where sUSDai yield comes from
  > sUSDai earns from three sources: interest on performing loans, interest on loans in escrow, and yield on stablecoin reserves.
  > Performing loans: 3.63%
  > Loans in escrow: 1.59%
  > Stablecoin reserves: 2.85%
  > Total gross: 8.07%
  > Net to holders, after protocol fees: 6.89%
  > sUSDai holders have earned $15.2 million after fees: $8.5 million from GPU loans and $6.7 million from PYUSD reserve yield.
  > Loans took over. Loans earned almost nothing in February and about two thirds of September's $2.63 million ($1.76 million).
  > Monthly earnings doubled, from about $1.3 million in March to $2.6 million in September.
  > Realized yield after fees rose, from about 5.2% in April to 6.0% in August and 6.6% in September.
  > October 2: first loan sale through GLX. The $98.1 million Duos Edge AI facility was sold to an institutional buyer, and loans on the books moved from $401 million to $311 million. More on GLX in the announcement.
  > Points
  > About 904 billion points were earned from deposits, or about 955 billion Allo points including referrals.
  > Daily points grew 1.6x, from about 3.51 billion in the first week to 5.73 billion in the last week.
  > Where points were earned
  > Pendle and Fluid earned about 87% of deposit points between them.
  > From late May, sUSDai smart collateral on Fluid (T2/T4) became the largest single source, about 36% of each day's points.
  > Solana went live in September. Jupiter Lend now earns about 10% of daily points, and Morpho borrowing against sUSDai ramped up the same month.
  > Top 100 wallets
  > The top 100 wallets earned 699 billion points, about 73% of all Allo points including referrals: 669 billion from strategies and 30.3 billion from referrals. Their strategy mix:
  > sUSDai smart collateral (Fluid T2/T4): 34.8%
  > sUSDai single asset collateral: 26.0%
  > sUSDai Pendle YT: 17.5%
  > USDai Pendle YT: 12.3%
  > sUSDai in wallet: 2.6%
  > USDai in wallet: 0.6%
  > USDai Pendle LP: 0.4%
  > Other: 5.8%
  > Season 3: Metropolitan
  > Season 3 introduces the CHIP Hurdle Rate: a 7% minimum yield for sUSDai. Whenever sUSDai yield falls below 7%, the Yield Reserve tops it up to 7%.
  > sUSDai yield comes from interest on GPU loans and from the stablecoin reserves held between loans. With GLX, seasoned loans are sold to institutions and the capital is recycled into new loans. Between a sale and the next loan, that capital sits in stablecoin reserves, which earn less than loans. The CHIP Hurdle Rate keeps holder yield steady through those cycles.
  > Season 3 starts at 00:00 UTC on October 15, immediately after the Season 2 snapshot, and runs six months to April 14, 2027. Season 3 points start from zero and share a separate CHIP pool.
  > Goals
  > Ultra sound yield. sUSDai that is highly liquid, with reliable mechanics and a competitive yield, and reducing dependence on CHIP emissions to harden CHIP economics.
  > GLX. Growing loan volume and turnover through the GPU Loan Exchange, where seasoned loans are sold to institutions and the capital is recycled into new loans.
  > What earns points
  > USDai and sUSDai earn points in any qualifying position: in your wallet, as collateral on lending markets, in Pendle and Exponent, and in liquidity pools. CHIP liquidity pools also earn points. There is no minimum balance and no cap. See the full list on the Opportunities page.
  > Multipliers
  > Each multiplier is the points earned per day for every $1 in the position. The lowest is 1x.
  > Locked CHIP and sCHIP do not earn Season 3 points.
  > Referrals
  > Referral boosts are removed for Season 3. In Season 2 they were mostly self referrals, which added points without bringing in new users.
  > FAQ
  > What is the CHIP Hurdle Rate?
  > Starting in Season 3, the CHIP Hurdle Rate sets a 7% minimum yield for sUSDai. If the yield from loans and stablecoin reserves falls below 7%, the difference is paid from the Yield Reserve, so holders still earn at least 7%. sUSDai earns 6.89% net today. The Yield Reserve is funded by the treasury at first, and over time by GLX gain-on-sale revenue.
  > When does Season 2 end?
  > Points stop accruing on October 14 at 11:59 p.m. UTC.
  > Do I need to withdraw or move anything?
  > No. Keep your positions where they are. Qualifying positions start earning Season 3 points right after the snapshot.
  > Do Season 2 points carry over to Season 3?
  > No. Season 2 points convert to CHIP from the 225 million CHIP Season 2 allocation. Season 3 points start from zero and share a separate pool.
  > When will I know how much CHIP I'm getting?
  > From October 15, the app shows your final Season 2 points and an estimated CHIP amount. Final allocations are published October 19.
  > What if my points look wrong?
  > Contact the team in the Telegram channel or Support Chat by October 19.

- **2026-10-26** · ♥ 27 · ↻ 1 — **Article: Why Reinsurance Yield Is Largely Uncorrelated with Stocks, Bonds, and Crypto** [post](https://x.com/re/status/2114740445964324949)
  > Experienced investors know that markets can swing fast and swing together. For example, the 2008 upheaval in US mortgage markets helped trigger crashes in global stock markets. More recently, concerns over inflation and energy prices due to the war in Iran have impacted markets from stocks to crypto.
  > Unfortunately, this degree of correlation is a feature, rather than a bug, of finance. Investments that look very different and depend on very different sources can be exposed to the same economic pressures.
  > Some sources of yield, however, are less closely tied to market movements. Reinsurance, for example, is largely uncorrelated with them: it depends instead chiefly upon the performance of the actual underlying insurance operations.
  > But what does it mean for returns to be largely uncorrelated? What drives the likes of stocks, bonds, and crypto? Why is reinsurance different? And why hasn’t it seen more widespread adoption by investors? Let’s explore.
  > What is correlation?
  > Correlation describes how closely investment returns move together. Investments that tend to rise and fall together are positively correlated. Some investments may be strongly correlated because of the sector they're in, or because they respond similarly to changes in interest rates; for example, share prices across a range of industries can drop concurrently if investors expect a widespread slowdown in consumer spending. Because different asset classes can respond to the same market forces, correlation can extend across markets.
  > That's important for diversification. If you're holding several categories of assets but they're all strongly correlated, then you're lacking the protection that diversity is intended to provide: a major market event could negatively impact those assets at the same time.
  > When investments are largely uncorrelated, the performance of one will track that of the other very little, if at all.
  > Correlation within and across markets
  > Correlation can exist in assets both within a given market and across different markets. Stocks, bonds, and crypto each offer examples of how ostensibly diversified assets can respond concurrently to the same market pressures.
  > Stocks
  > Stock prices reflect what investors are willing to pay for a share of a business, and that depends in part on what they expect the business to earn. Those expectations can change for reasons well beyond the company itself. An otherwise well-run business might face higher fuel costs, more expensive borrowing, or customers with less money to spend. Investors may then expect lower profits and become less willing to pay the same price for its shares.
  > That helps explain why stocks in different sectors can fall together. Higher oil prices, for example, can increase costs for both an airline and a manufacturer, despite the differences between their businesses. A broader economic slowdown can reduce demand across many industries at once. The companies haven't become identical investments; they're simply exposed to the same problem.
  > Bonds
  > Bond investors lend money in exchange for specified payments. But those payments don't fix the price at which the bond can be sold. Suppose an investor holds a bond paying 3% interest, and comparable new bonds begin offering 5%. A buyer has little reason to pay the same price for the lower-paying bond, so its market value falls. A rise in interest rates can therefore reduce the value of many existing fixed-rate bonds simultaneously.
  > Borrowers' ability to repay also matters. An economic downturn can weaken the finances of multiple companies, making investors less confident that they'll receive the payments promised. Bonds issued by different businesses can consequently fall together, particularly where those businesses face similar financial difficulties.
  > Crypto
  > Crypto prices can change because of developments within a particular project, but sentiment can also spread across the industry. Investors becoming less willing to hold crypto can reduce demand for multiple tokens at once, including those belonging to projects that haven't experienced a specific setback.
  > Crypto’s trading mechanics can amplify that selling. Investors who have borrowed to increase their exposure may be forced to close positions as prices fall. Those liquidations create further selling, which can push prices down again. Holding several different tokens can therefore leave an investor exposed to a shared decline in demand and the selling that follows.
  > How those connections extend across markets
  > The same economic development can affect all three asset classes through different mechanisms. Rising interest rates can make borrowing more expensive for businesses, reducing expected profits and the prices investors will pay for their stocks. They can also reduce the resale value of existing fixed-rate bonds. Meanwhile, higher interest payments available elsewhere can make investors less willing to hold speculative assets such as crypto. The investments differ, but their prices can still fall in response to the same change in conditions.
  > Is reinsurance yield correlated with stocks, bonds, or crypto?
  > Reinsurance yield, the source of return behind Re, is largely uncorrelated with stocks, bonds, and crypto. Reinsurance is a major market in its own right, but it is driven by different forces than the primary financial markets. Underwriting performance depends almost exclusively upon the underlying insurance operations.
  > For the reinsurance-derived return, that means performance is driven by a simple formula: premiums earned from insurers on the one side, and the sum of claims and expenses on the other.
  > Underwriting performance isn't driven by sentiment, or speculation, or profit projections: market events that cause a fall in stock prices or a crypto sell-off typically have no real impact upon the premiums earned or the claims owed under reinsurance contracts.
  > Why isn't reinsurance in more portfolios?
  > All of the above raises an obvious question: why isn’t reinsurance ubiquitous in portfolios already?
  > The answer is simple: for the vast majority of its existence as a market, reinsurance has been almost exclusively the realm of a small number of major players. The steep capital requirements and need for industry relationships have set a prohibitively high barrier to entry.
  > Re changes that.
  > How does Re provide access to reinsurance yield?
  > Re democratizes access by sourcing capital from the DeFi markets. Participants deposit stablecoins to mint reUSD or reUSDe, yield-bearing tokens. A portion of user capital is deployed offchain to back reinsurance treaties, and underwriting revenue flows back to the protocol to support token yield. In short: individuals bypass the capital barriers, skip the industry relationships, and gain access to the market.
  > For a closer look at correlation across DeFi yield in particular, read Re and the Uncorrelated Market.
  > Explore Re →

- **2026-10-19** · ♥ 42 · ↻ 4 — **Article: Resilience Foundation Deploys $65M to Cover Re** [post](https://x.com/re/status/2112261615991267462)
  > Re has undertaken its second major deployment of onchain capital into the reinsurance market.
  > The Resilience Foundation, operator of Re Protocol, executed a new $65 million investment note with Cover Re SPC Ltd. Cover Re is a licensed, Cayman Islands-based reinsurer.
  > The transaction follows an initial $100 million investment in April, bringing the total deployed to Cover Re through the two notes to $165 million.
  > From onchain capital to offchain reinsurance
  > The capital deployed in the note was sourced from onchain deposits. When users mint reUSD or reUSDe, they contribute to the capital pool. The value of the capital pool also increases as each token’s net asset value (NAV) increases.
  > Re periodically deploys a portion of that capital offchain to Cover Re, which converts it from stablecoins to US dollars. Cover Re uses the capital to support new reinsurance contracts.
  > All of Cover Re's reinsurance contracts are fully collateralized; this means that it posts assets to fully back its contractual obligations, providing financial security to the ceding insurers. The collateral is held in segregated trust accounts.
  > Payments on the investment notes, sourced from Cover Re's reinsurance activities, flow back onchain and support yield for holders.
  > What this means for Cover Re
  > For Cover Re, the infusion of capital increases underwriting capacity: it allows the reinsurer to add new business and expand its portfolio.
  > In September, the company added three new reinsurance treaties covering specialty auto, professional lines for startups, and large excess casualty. Those treaties brought Cover Re's total bound premium since inception to more than $515 million; this figure represents the cumulative premiums associated with Cover Re's reinsurance contracts.
  > What it means for holders
  > For holders of reUSD and reUSDe, offchain deployment of capital is a key part of the cycle that supports yield. Yield, in turn, incrementally increases token NAV.
  > reUSD and reUSDe each earn a protocol-determined spread (2.5% for the former and 8.5% for the latter, as of October 2026). Offchain reUSD and reUSDe also earn a base rate tied to SOFR, a benchmark for overnight borrowing backed by US Treasuries. Cover Re's reinsurance operations fund both components of that yield.
  > What to look for
  > Visitors to the Re App will notice a substantial decrease in onchain capital and an increase in offchain capital. This reflects capital transferred offchain to fund the note.
  > reUSDe is intended to be deployed offchain almost in its entirety for use as collateral (a small portion remains onchain to fund future redemption windows). At the time of any offchain deployment, at least 50% of reUSD’s all-time peak deposit level remains onchain to fund instant redemptions; the rest may be deployed offchain as collateral.

- **2026-10-14** · ♥ 25 · ↻ 4 — **Article: What Does a Reinsurance Broker Do? Interview w/ Howden Re Managing Director of Broking Josephine Li** [post](https://x.com/re/status/2110414854049976750)
  > Insurance is a critical component of life in today's world. You buy insurance to protect yourself and your assets, be that health, your car, your home, your possessions, and more. Businesses and even governments do the same.
  > But insurers need insurance too. That’s why they seek out reinsurance: insurance for insurers. Hardly anyone outside of the insurance world will ever see it, but it’s critical to the industry at large.
  > When insurers seek reinsurance, it’s the reinsurance brokers who do the legwork. They’re involved from start to finish in arranging the deals, and their work continues well after the contract is signed.
  > Josephine Li, ACAS, is managing director of broking at Howden Re, one of the world’s leading reinsurance brokerages. In this interview, she discusses the role of a broker, what a deal involves, and her experience working with Cover Re.
  > The work and the person behind it
  > The role of a reinsurance broker draws on expertise in risk, negotiation, analysis, and industry knowledge. Brokers act as the primary channel through which insurers seek out reinsurance, helping them determine suitable reinsurers, negotiate the terms, and then manage the ongoing work after a contract is signed. Josephine’s experience offers a view into the profession, including her path into broking, what the work involves, and the lessons she’s learned about building relationships and a career in reinsurance.
  > For readers who’ve never met a reinsurance broker, how do you describe your job at a dinner party?
  > We help insurance companies insure themselves. A reinsurance broker is part strategist, part translator and part negotiator. We help clients understand the risks they’re taking, decide how much of that risk they want to keep, and find capital to take the rest.
  > What does a typical day actually look like for you right now?
  > There really isn’t one. I can go from talking with a client about growth or capital strategy, to working through analytics and treaty structure, to negotiating with reinsurers, to getting different internal teams aligned around a placement. A lot of the job is connecting the technical, commercial and relationship pieces.
  > You have an actuarial background. How does someone go from the actuarial track to broking, and what made you make that jump?
  > The actuarial background gave me a very strong foundation for deconstructing risk and testing whether the economics make sense. What attracted me to broking was being able to take that analytical foundation and add strategy, negotiation, clients and the market. I liked being closer to the decisions rather than only analyzing them after the fact.
  > What’s the moment you knew reinsurance was going to be your career? Was there a deal, a mentor, a market event?
  > It wasn’t one dramatic moment. I stumbled into reinsurance after college, but I stayed because the problems kept being interesting and no two deals were ever exactly the same.
  > What’s the hardest lesson the market has taught you? A deal that went sideways, a renewal you lost, a call you got wrong?
  > Being technically right isn’t enough. You have to understand what motivates everyone involved, communicate clearly, get the timing right and know when to push versus when to listen. And when you do get something wrong, owning it quickly matters a lot more than trying to defend it.
  > Reinsurance is a relationship business. How did you build your book of relationships when you were starting out, before you had a track record?
  > By being useful. When you’re early in your career, you may not have decades of relationships or a big book, but you can be prepared, responsive, curious and dependable. If people learn that when they call you they’ll get a thoughtful answer and you’ll do what you said you were going to do, the relationships build from there.
  > What’s a misconception about brokers (even inside the industry) that you’d like to correct?
  > That the job is primarily matchmaking, take a risk from a client and find someone willing to write it. The best broking is much more consultative than that. A good broker should be helping shape the risk, challenge assumptions, improve the structure, create competition and ultimately help both sides make a better decision.
  > What advice would you give someone considering a move into broking today?
  > Learn the fundamentals first. Understand analytics, contracts, how your client makes money and how the reinsurer makes money. Then learn how to explain complicated things simply. The people who can combine technical credibility with judgment, communication and relationships become very valuable very quickly.
  > How a reinsurance deal comes together
  > A reinsurance deal begins with establishing the insurer’s specific goals. The broker then works through the insurer’s data, develops potential contract structures, and approaches potential reinsurers to seek feedback and negotiate. After the agreement is signed, the broker continues working with both sides on contract issues, amendments, and premium and claims activity throughout the life of the treaty.
  > Josephine describes how that process unfolds in practice: the preparation behind a deal, and the factors she considers when selecting a reinsurer.
  > Walk me through a deal from the very first conversation to binding. Where does a treaty actually begin (a phone call, a renewal date, a lunch)?
  > The treaty usually starts with a business problem, not a treaty structure. You first understand what the client is trying to accomplish, growth, capital relief, earnings protection, something else. Then you work through the data and modeling, develop structures, build the underwriting story, approach the right markets, take feedback, negotiate terms and capacity, allocate the line and ultimately bind it. The structure is really the output of understanding the underlying problem.
  > Matchmaking is the visible part of broking. What’s the invisible part, like the work clients and reinsurers never see?
  > A huge amount of preparation. It’s understanding the portfolio, pressure-testing the analytics, anticipating reinsurer questions, figuring out which markets are genuinely relevant, managing competing interests and sometimes solving problems before the client ever knows they existed. The meeting or negotiation is the visible 10%; most of the work happened before anyone got into the room.
  > When you’re deciding which reinsurer to bring a client’s risk to, what are you actually weighing? What separates a market you trust from one you don’t?
  > Price and capacity obviously matter, but they’re only part of it. I’m looking at underwriting appetite, financial strength, expertise, responsiveness, consistency and whether I believe they’ll still behave like a partner when something goes wrong. A market earns trust through what it does after the easy part of the transaction is over.
  > Once a treaty is bound, you service it for its whole life. What does that actually involve month to month, and why does it matter so much?
  > Binding isn’t the end of the relationship. There are contract issues, data, premium and loss activity, claims, amendments, changing exposures and ongoing conversations with both sides throughout the year. Good servicing also makes the next renewal better because nobody wants to rediscover the relationship once a year.
  > Working with Cover Re
  > Howden Re is Cover Re’s largest broker relationship. Josephine shares her first impressions of Cover Re, her experience working with its technical team, and what Cover Re has done to secure repeat business with Howden.
  > Do you remember the first Cover Re deal you worked on? What was your honest first impression of them as a market?
  > What stood out was that you were innovative without treating innovation as a substitute for underwriting. There are plenty of new ideas around capital and technology, but ultimately this business still comes down to understanding risk, pricing it appropriately and honoring your commitments.
  > Is there a moment in the relationship that stands out?
  > The team’s technical capability stands out, especially in structured markets. Jon [Lim, chief underwriting officer) and Blanca [Qin, head of underwriting] are very strong technicians in their respective areas, which makes conversations about structure mechanics much smoother. It feels more like a thoughtful debate with knowledgeable peers than having to explain how each part of the deal mechanics works.

- **2026-10-13** · ♥ 32 · ↻ 0 — **Article: Claims, Costs, and Premiums: Understanding Reinsurance Treaty Performance** [post](https://x.com/re/status/2110016837148917880)
  > Reinsurance, like any other area of finance, is a for-profit business. Reinsurers carefully select, price, and negotiate treaties with an eye toward one primary goal: fulfilling their obligations to the insurer while maximizing margins. For the average reinsurer, that means greater value for shareholders. For Re, underwriting profits support yield for holders of reUSD and reUSDe.
  > But what goes into determining the profit equation? What costs and revenues go into it? And how is the result measured? Let’s explore.
  > The Treaty
  > Before any money changes hands, the terms of the deal have to be set. That's the role of the reinsurance treaty: the contract between insurer and reinsurer. It determines what business is covered, how much risk the reinsurer takes on, how the reinsurer will be compensated for taking that risk, and under what circumstances it will be responsible for losses.
  > Some treaties may include caps on what the reinsurer is expected to pay, while others will not. Some require reinsurers to pay out a portion of covered losses from the start, whereas others may require insured losses to reach a certain level first. The exact details can vary widely, but the basic purpose is the same.
  > The Considerations
  > When deciding upon a treaty, the reinsurer must answer two questions: is this risk worth taking? And if so, what premium should we charge for it?
  > A lot goes into this decision. For example:
  > Historical loss data: how often has this type of claim occurred, and what did it cost when it did?
  > Risk models: statistical tools that estimate the probability and severity of future losses.
  > Exposure data: the specific characteristics of the risk being covered (such as vehicle types, geographic area, industry classification, or workforce size).
  > Market benchmarks: what are other reinsurers charging for similar risks, and why?
  > Let’s look at commercial auto, Re’s second largest category of business (as of October 2026). Before backing commercial auto business, reinsurance underwriters will analyze the likes of fleet size, vehicle types, and their primary use; operating locations (urban routes generally see more accidents than rural ones); the insurer’s claims history over the long term; and the financial health of the insurer itself.
  > The goal is to build an accurate picture of how likely claims are to occur, how expensive they will be, and, above all, whether the premium justifies the risk. The premium must be high enough to cover expected claims and operating costs yet still generate a good return for the reinsurers.
  > In the end, the performance of a treaty comes down to a simple comparison: how much money the reinsurer earned in premiums versus the amount it had to pay out in costs.
  > The Costs
  > Claims
  > When one thinks about insurance (and thus, by relation, about reinsurance), the primary cost one probably considers is claims. Policyholders file claims when covered events occur, and the insurer pays out based on the terms of the policy. It’s much the same for reinsurers; when events covered by a reinsurance treaty transpire, the reinsurer pays the insurer based on the letter of the contract between them.
  > But the claims category doesn't just include the cost of paying out those claims. It also includes the costs associated with those claims. For example, processing claims could require paying for defense lawyers, expert witnesses, fraud investigators, forensic accountants, court costs, and so on. There’s also the overhead of having a claims department at all: staff salaries, general infrastructure, even the office they work in.
  > Other Expenses
  > In addition to claims, there are the more ordinary expenses. Running an enterprise costs money, from the investment involved in finding business to the more mundane costs of operation. These are what you’d expect: they range from the likes of rent, utilities, and building maintenance, to furniture, equipment, computers and software, to cloud hosting, tax preparation, outside legal counsel, and staff salaries. They may also extend to reinsurance broker fees, costs associated with treaty negotiation, financial audits, and so on.
  > The Revenue
  > Revenue from reinsurance contracts comes from insurer premiums. Just as insurers receive premiums from policyholders for providing coverage, so do reinsurers from insurers. How much the reinsurer is paid depends on the terms of the treaty and can vary widely.
  > Premiums represent the revenue side of the underwriting performance equation. When determining underwriting performance, all costs are measured against them.
  > The Results
  > This brings us to the most important metric in reinsurance economics: the combined ratio. It’s the chief indicator of underwriting profitability (or lack thereof).
  > Combined ratio is a simple equation: add together claims and other expenses, then divide that sum by premiums taken in, and convert the result into a percentage.
  > Let’s say that for a given treaty, claims equaled $5 million, other expenses added another $4 million, and premiums were $10 million. That would yield a combined ratio of 90%, indicating that an underwriting profit was made. In simple terms, the reinsurer earned a dollar of premium for every 90 cents it spent on the treaty.
  > It’s rare for a reinsurer to carry just a single treaty. Reinsurers vary widely in size; most may carry dozens, hundreds, or even (in the case of the real titans) thousands of treaties at any given time. For any reinsurer, the most important manifestation of combined ratio isn’t the performance of any given treaty but rather that of their portfolio as a whole. That bundles together the performance of all treaties in the reinsurer’s book. 100% means that the reinsurer broke even. Over 100% equals a loss. Less than 100% means an underwriting profit.
  > Re’s Performance
  > To date, Re’s portfolio has been profitable (that is, a combined ratio of below 100%) in each individual year since inception. Since inception, it has recorded an overall combined ratio of 92%¹.
  > 92%¹ Overall combined ratio since inception
  > Several factors help to explain that performance. First, a skilled and diligent underwriting team. Second, favoring meaningfully high loss thresholds (where applicable) before Re begins paying for losses. And finally, a highly diversified portfolio with only modest exposure to catastrophe.
  > ¹ Past performance is not a reliable indicator of future results.
  > Read the Data
  > Traditional reinsurance is opaque. Treaty details rarely travel much beyond the insurer and reinsurer. They almost invariably aren’t available to the public.
  > Re takes a different approach. Those who provide capital to the portfolio have a detailed view into how that capital is being utilized, from overall strategy to details and performance data on each individual treaty, including premium, posted collateral, projected losses, and combined ratio.
  > For that information and more, check out the Re App.

- **2026-10-08** · ♥ 27 · ↻ 0 — **Article: Performance Update: September 2026** [post](https://x.com/re/status/2108291837622710747)
  > September brought two more major milestones for Re: total deposits across reUSD and reUSDe passed $300 million, and cumulative yield generated for holders exceeded $10 million.
  > The month also brought continued expansion on both sides of the business. Re added three new lines of business to its reinsurance portfolio, while reUSD added new markets across Exponent and Fluid.
  > $712.25M - Total Value Locked
  > +9.8% - TVL Growth
  > $318.4M - Total Deposits
  > $10M+ - Cumulative Yield Generated
  > Capital Position & Liquidity
  > All figures reflect the September 30, 2026, reporting snapshot unless otherwise noted. Past performance is not a reliable indicator of future results.
  > Re’s TVL stood at $712.25 million on September 30, an increase of 9.8% over August.
  > As of the reporting period, TVL was composed as follows:
  > $210.14M (29.5% of total assets) is held onchain to support Re-backed underwriting structures.
  > $183.18M (25.7% of total) is maintained offchain as reserves within regulated insurance entities.
  > $318.93M (44.8% of total) is composed of contracted written premium receivable. (Reflects contracted premium receivable on policies bound as of the reporting date. Actual receipts may vary based on policy performance, cessions, and other factors.)
  > As in August, capital growth was driven primarily by large-scale gains in reUSD deposits.
  > Deposits Passed $300 Million
  > August was marked by strong growth in total deposits. September saw that trend continue. Total deposits across reUSD and reUSDe increased from $256.4 million at the end of August to $318.4 million at the end of September, a gain of $62.0 million (24.2%).
  > Total Deposits: $256.4M → $318.4M
  > reUSD: $236.4M → $297.8M
  > reUSDe: $20.0M → $20.6M
  > The increase was driven in its vast majority by deposits in reUSD, which grew from $236.4 million to $297.8 million. reUSDe deposits increased from $20.0 million to $20.6 million.
  > Total deposits measure the combined value of all reUSD and reUSDe in circulation. Unlike TVL, the figure excludes premium receivable and capital held outside the circulating token supply.
  > Total Yield Generated Exceeded $10 Million
  > September included another milestone: cumulative yield generated for reUSD and reUSDe holders passed the $10 million mark.
  > Both tokens accrue yield through increases in NAV. Yield is primarily driven by licensed reinsurance activity.
  > Deposits measure the capital users have brought into the protocol; cumulative yield provides a separate measure of what that capital has generated for holders over time.
  > Portfolio Composition
  > Re added three new treaties during September, expanding Re’s underwriting portfolio into three additional areas of business:
  > Specialty auto - insurance for business vehicles and fleets with specialized risks that may not fit standard commercial auto coverage
  > Professional lines for startups - insurance covering business risks such as professional mistakes, management decisions, and other liabilities faced by startups
  > Large excess casualty - insurance for very large liability claims against businesses, including major accidents, injuries, and lawsuits
  > The new treaties brought inception-to-date bound premium to $510.6 million.
  > The addition of new categories further diversifies Re’s portfolio. Greater diversification reduces the chances that losses in any one area will coincide with losses elsewhere, limiting the impact that poor performance in any single category of business can have on the portfolio as a whole.
  > Though these new lines involve longer-duration risks, they make up only a small share of Re’s portfolio. Underwriting strategy continues to favor two primary characteristics: shorter-duration risks, and contracts that only begin paying after losses cross a meaningful threshold. This strategy is intended to help guard against losses and emphasize consistency in underwriting performance.
  > Ecosystem Expansion
  > September also brought additions to reUSD markets.
  > @ExponentFinance: A December 2026 reUSD market went live on Exponent, including PT-reUSD for fixed-rate exposure through maturity, plus YT-reUSD and a reUSD liquidity pool.
  > @0xFluid: Re expanded its reUSD integration with Fluid on Arbitrum. New vaults went live for reUSD/USDC, reUSD/USDT, and reUSD-USDC LP/USDC.
  > $RE Token Activity
  > Per the standard ecosystem release schedule, 7.09 million $RE unlocked from the ecosystem allocation on September 18.
  > $RE has a fixed maximum supply of one billion tokens, with no ongoing inflation or perpetual emissions.
  > 7.09M - $RE released, ecosystem allocation · September 18
  > 1B - Fixed maximum supply, no ongoing inflation
  > What’s Next
  > October 1 saw the opening of the second reUSDe redemption window. Redemption requests will be accepted through October 14, with redemptions claimable from October 15 through November 14.
  > We look forward to delivering our next update in November.
  > - Re Management Team

- **2026-10-08** · ♥ 50 · ↻ 1 — Moody’s Ratings has assigned a counterparty risk rating to Sky Protocol, the first stablecoin protocol to receive a rating from the credit agency. Sky Protocol has become the first stablecoin protocol rated by both Moody’s Ratings and S&P Global Ratings. Two independent assessments give institutional allocators established credit-rating frameworks to evaluate Sky Protocol’s financial position, operations and governance. Moody’s identified Sky Reserves as a central factor in its assessment. Reserve building remains a priority, supported by the strengthened Treasury Management Function and Stage 2 buyback and reserves framework, which retains a portion of Net Protocol Surplus toward a governance-set minimum reserve target. Progress is publicly trackable through the Financial Dashboard. We appreciate Moody’s rigorous review. Sky Governance and Sky Frontier Foundation are reviewing both agencies’ findings to inform next steps toward stronger creditworthiness, which will be published as part of the forthcoming roadmap. - **2026-10-27** · ♥ 84 · ↻ 6 — **Article: Connecting Morpho Vaults to Morpho Midnight** [post](https://x.com/Morpho/status/2115096277645975723)
  > Morpho Vaults V2 (“Morpho Vaults”) are expanding the types of credit they can allocate to.
  > Today, Morpho Vaults allocate to variable-rate Morpho Blue markets. The Midnight Adapter will go live in Q4 2026, enabling all new and existing Morpho Vaults to allocate deposits into fixed rate Morpho Midnight (“Midnight”) Markets.
  > This opens Midnight to the $5B+ already held in Morpho Vaults. For lenders, it means that vaults can offer more predictable, fixed-rate yield for a set term, without having to manage positions themselves. For vaults, it opens up a new design space and greater differentiation.
  > New Design Space & Differentiation for Vaults
  > On Blue, vaults stand out through the markets they choose and the risk settings they use. Curators are largely choosing between the same underlying market structure and differentiating through market selection, allocation weight, and rebalancing.
  > The Midnight adapter changes that materially, opening up a new design space for Morpho Vaults:
  > Two market structures instead of one: By opening access to Midnight, vaults can be configured to allocate across both market structures (Blue & Midnight). This gives curators more ways to show the value of their vault setup.
  > Utilization and Liquidity Profile: Curators can allocate across Blue, Midnight and idle markets to shape a vault's utilization and liquidity profile. Midnight's fixed-term positions can enable a higher utilization and potentially high returns, but high utilization does not necessarily mean less is withdrawable. Blue allocations can benefit from shared market liquidity and keep a vault highly withdrawable, while Midnight allocations are shaped by their terms and maturities. Curators can use these different liquidity profiles to differentiate their vaults.
  > Vaults can set their own terms: On Morpho Blue, vaults receive the rate set by the market’s interest rate model. With Morpho Midnight, there is no formula. The curator configures the risk parameters and the given rate at which the vault is willing to accept.
  > The result is vault infrastructure that enables a high level of differentiation, not just from the type of collateral assets, but in pricing, term, and liquidity.
  > How Morpho Vaults handle Midnight positions
  > Midnight fixed-rate positions behave differently from variable-rate Blue positions. The design challenge was to enable the allocation from Morpho Vaults to Midnight while preserving the vaults’ noncustodial properties and user protections. As a result, the Midnight Adapter enforces the following:
  > Buying protections: A vault’s Midnight positions must stay within its exposure and maturity limits.
  > Selling protections: By default, a vault cannot sell a whole position for less than the amortized value. An exception to this would be subject to a timelock.
  > Caps: Every Midnight position counts against the vault’s caps on the things that define its risk, such as the collateral asset and its parameters. Curators can also cap how much of the vault is lent for longer periods, for example beyond 90 days. Caps can be lowered immediately, but increasing them is timelocked, so depositors can exit before any increase in risk applies.
  > In-kind redemptions: Users can exit a vault position into the underlying Midnight position, even when the vault has no idle liquidity, through in-kind redemptions.
  > Amortized valuation: Each position is valued at the amount due at maturity, discounted at its entry rate. Its value accrues steadily as maturity approaches.
  > Together, these design choices keep Morpho Vaults noncustodial and put predefined limits on the risks a vault can take with Midnight positions.
  > A defining change for Morpho Vaults & Midnight
  > The Midnight adapter is an important step in expanding Morpho Vaults beyond a single market structure, and opening $5B in liquidity to Midnight Markets.
  > Morpho Vaults will soon be able to allocate liquidity across both Morpho Blue’s open-ended, variable-rate markets and Midnight’s fixed-rate, fixed-term markets, giving users a choice of vaults with different liquidity and variable yield profiles.

- **2026-10-08** · ♥ 63 · ↻ 5 — **Article: Morpho Effect September: Noncustodiality and Vaults** [post](https://x.com/Morpho/status/2108177023159898584)
  > Curation is a high growth early-stage industry.
  > Two and a half years ago it barely existed as a business. Today roughly $10B sits in curated vaults.
  > Coinbase and Robinhood now run their earn products on them, and each passed $500M in deposits in September.
  > More than 30 curators are now active, including established names like Steakhouse Financial, Sentora, Gauntlet, and recent entrants like Galaxy Digital, Bitwise Asset Management and Armitage by Wintermute.
  > Underneath sit the vaults: the onchain infrastructure curators use to set risk parameters and allocation rules. A year ago we argued that vaults would become the default way to allocate liquidity onchain. That has become the consensus, and institutions are moving into the space.
  > But a vault has come to mean very different things, depending on the power of its curator. We think of it as a spectrum. On one end are noncustodial vaults, where the curator sets the rules but cannot change allocation without users consent or take control of user assets. On the other end are discretionary vaults, where the operator custody customer assets, and can move assets as they see fit to run more complex strategies.
  > Despite Morpho Vaults being designed as noncustodial, with the combination of in-kind redemptions, timelocks, role-based access controls and immutable contracts, the same open-source infrastructure could also be forked and configured to create something that looks more like a discretionary vault.
  > At the end of the day, there is no one size fits all. Different needs will require different vault infrastructure. The more diversity we have at the vault infrastructure layer, the more tools we have to bring all types of credit onchain. We believe the more diverse the vault ecosystem is, the more tools we have to bring all types of credit onchain.
  > More on Morpho Midnight (and a little on Blue)
  > Coinbase fixed rate loans, powered by Morpho
  > Coinbase is the first large-scale distributor to integrate Morpho Midnight only a few months after launch, demonstrating trust in the security of the protocol. Millions of Coinbase customers can now take out fixed rateloans alongside the already successful variable rate loans powered by Morpho Blue.
  > P2P Orders on Midnight
  > An offer-based model also lets two counterparties settle a loan directly, without routing through an existing pool. That suits allocators or borrowers who want bespoke terms and speed. With P2P Orders on Morpho Midnight, a user makes an offer on their own terms, sends it to their chosen counterparty and both parties settle directly on Morpho Midnight.
  > P2P Orders are available on @Ethereum, @Base, and @Arc, with support for more networks coming.
  > Midnight is live on Ethereum
  > @Ethereum is most neutral network with one of the deepest liquidity onchain. It brings more Midnight's fixed rate markets to users who have a mandate to allocate on Ethereum only. Midnight's crosschain expansion has just begun.
  > Direct Lend into Morpho Blue
  > Lending directly into Morpho Blue markets has always been possible by interacting with the smart contracts or through third-party apps such as Monarch Lend.
  > It is now available in the Morpho App, for users who are comfortable applying their own risk expertise.
  > Morpho is the credit infrastructure on Arc
  > Morpho is live on Arc, Circle's Economic OS for the internet, where it powers credit use cases.
  > Deposits reached $220M+ on day one and topped $500M at the time of writing. Digital Asset Borrow via Circle Mint is also powered by Morpho, with close to $200M borrowed in under two weeks.
  > Ecosystem news
  > Stock-backed loans on Coinbase Tokenized Stocks are live on Morpho Blue and Midnight
  > Coinbase Lend passed $500M
  > Robinhood Earn passed $500M
  > Morpho on Robinhood Chain reached $1B in deposits in three months
  > Zama doubles down on confidential vaults on Morpho launching 16 additional confidential vaults
  > Turnkey shipped an embedded earn API powered by Morpho
  > OUSD lending is coming to integrators on Tempo
  > Morpho curators growths
  > Steakhouse Financial's assets under curation have exceeded $2.3B
  > Bitwise's first vault is live
  > Sentora's assets under curation passed $1B, from close to zero at the start of 2026. Here is why Sentora curates on Morpho.
  > Morpho total deposits at an all-time high
  > Total deposits passed $15B in mid-September and sits at nearly $17B at the time of writing.
